Optimal Controls for Forward-Backward Stochastic Differential Equations: Time-Inconsistency and Time-Consistent Solutions
Hanxiao Wang Jiongmin Yong Chao Zhou

Abstract




Abstract.
This paper is concerned with an optimal control problem for a forward-backward stochastic differential equation
(FBSDE, for short) with a recursive cost functional determined by a backward stochastic Volterra integral equation
(BSVIE, for short). It is found that such an optimal control problem is time-inconsistent in general,
even if the cost functional is reduced to a classical Bolza type one as in Peng
[
50
]
,
Lim–Zhou
[
41
]
, and Yong
[
74
]
. Therefore, instead of finding a global optimal control
(which is time-inconsistent), we will look for a time-consistent and locally optimal equilibrium strategy,
which can be constructed via the solution of an associated equilibrium Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB, for short) equation.
A verification theorem for the local optimality of the equilibrium strategy is proved by means of
the generalized Feynman–Kac formula for BSVIEs and some stability estimates of the representation for
parabolic partial differential equations (PDEs, for short). Under certain conditions,
it is proved that the equilibrium HJB equation, which is a nonlocal PDE, admits a unique classical solution.
As special cases and applications, the linear-quadratic problems, a mean-variance model,
a social planner problem with heterogeneous Epstein–Zin utilities,
and a Stackelberg game are briefly investigated.
It turns out that our framework can cover not only the optimal control problems for FBSDEs studied in
[
50
,
41
,
74
]
, and so on,
but also the problems of the general discounting and some nonlinear appearance of conditional expectations for the terminal state,
studied in Yong
[
75
,
77
]
and Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
.

Keywords.
Time-inconsistent optimal control problem,
controlled forward-backward stochastic differential equation,
backward stochastic Volterra integral equation,
equilibrium strategy, equilibrium Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation,
recursive utility, Feynman–Kac formula.

AMS subject classifications.
93E20, 49N70, 60H10, 60H20, 35K10, 49L20, 90C39.

1
Introduction

Let
(\Omega,{\cal F},\mathbb{P})
be a complete probability space on which a standard one-dimensional Brownian motion

W=\{W(t);0\leqslant t<\infty\}
is defined. The augmented natural filtration of
W(\cdot)
is denoted by
\mathbb{F}=\{{\cal F}_{t}\}_{t\geqslant 0}
.
Let
T>0
be a fixed time horizon. We denote

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}_{t}=L^{2}_{{\cal F}_{t}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})=\big\{\xi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}~|~\xi\hbox{ is ${\cal F}_{t}$-measurable, }\mathbb{E}[|\xi|^{2}]<\infty\big\},\quad t\in[0,T],

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}=\big\{(t,\xi)~|~t\in[0,T),\,\xi\in\mathscr{X}_{t}\big\},

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}[t,T]=\Big\{\varphi:[t,T]\times\Omega\to U\bigm|\varphi(\cdot)\hbox{~is $\mathbb{F}$-progressively measurable},~\mathbb{E}\int^{T}_{t}|\varphi(s)|^{2}ds<\infty\Big\},\quad t\in[0,T],

where
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
is a nonempty measurable set (either bounded or unbounded).
For any given
initial pair

(t,\xi)\in\mathscr{D}
and
control process

u(\cdot)\in\mathscr{U}[t,T]
,
consider the following controlled (decoupled) forward-backward stochastic differential equation (FBSDE, for short) on the time horizon
[t,T]
:

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle dX(s)=b(s,X(s),u(s))ds+\sigma(s,X(s),u(s))dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle dY(s)=-g(s,X(s),u(s),Y(s),Z(s))ds+Z(s)dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr X(t)=\xi,\quad Y(T)=h(X(T)),\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

where
b,\sigma:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
g:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
and
h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
are given deterministic mappings. Under certain mild conditions,
for any
(t,\xi)\in\mathscr{D}
and
u(\cdot)\in\mathscr{U}[t,T]
, (
1.1
) admits a unique adapted solution
(X(\cdot),Y(\cdot),Z(\cdot))\equiv\big(X(\cdot\,;t,\xi,u(\cdot)),Y(\cdot\,;t,\xi,u(\cdot)),Z(\cdot\,;t,\xi,u(\cdot))\big)
, which is called a
state process
.
To measure the performance of the control
u(\cdot)
, we introduce the following
recursive cost functional
:

J(t,\xi;u(\cdot))=Y^{0}(t),

(1.2)

where
Y^{0}(\cdot)
is uniquely determined by the following backward stochastic Volterra integral equation (BSVIE, for short):

\displaystyle Y^{0}(r)

\displaystyle=h^{0}(r,X(r),X(T),Y(r))+\int_{r}^{T}g^{0}(r,s,X(r),X(s),u(s),Y(s),Z(s),Y^{0}(s),Z^{0}(r,s))ds

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{r}^{T}Z^{0}(r,s)dW(s),\qquad\qquad r\in[t,T],

(1.3)

for which
(Y^{0}(\cdot),Z^{0}(\cdot\,,\cdot))
is the adapted solution. Here,
h^{0}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g^{0}:\Delta^{*}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are given deterministic mappings with

\Delta^{*}[0,T]=\big\{(t,s)\in[0,T]^{2}\bigm|0\leqslant t\leqslant s\leqslant T\big\}

(1.4)

being the upper triangle domain in the square
[0,T]^{2}
. Note that in the case that

h^{0}(r,\tilde{x},x,y)=h^{0}(x,y),\qquad g^{0}(r,s,\tilde{x},x,u,y,z,y^{0},z^{0})=g^{0}(s,x,u,y,z),

(1.5)

the recursive cost functional (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) is reduced to a Bolza type cost functional
for FBSDE state equation (see Peng
[
50
]
and Yong
[
74
]
, for examples):

J(t,\xi;u(\cdot))=\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[h^{0}(X(T),Y(t))+\int_{t}^{T}g^{0}(s,X(s),u(s),Y(s),Z(s))ds\Big],

(1.6)

where
\mathbb{E}_{t}[\,\cdot\,]=\mathbb{E}[\,\cdot\,|\mathscr{F}_{t}]
is the conditional expectation operator. Further, if

h^{0}(r,\tilde{x},x,y)=h^{0}(x),\qquad g^{0}(r,s,\tilde{x},x,u,y,z,y^{0},z^{0})=g^{0}(s,x,u),

(1.7)

then the cost functional is reduced to the most familiar classical Bolza functional:

J(t,\xi;u(\cdot))=\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[h^{0}(X(T))+\int_{t}^{T}g^{0}(s,X(s),u(s))ds\Big],

(1.8)

where the two terms on the right-hand side are called
terminal
and
running
costs, respectively.
Thus, our recursive cost functional is an extension of Bolza type cost functional.
With the state equation (
1.1
) and the recursive cost functional (
1.2
)–(
1.3
),
we may pose the following optimal control problem:

Problem (N)
. For any given initial pair
(t,\xi)\in\mathscr{D}
, find a control
\bar{u}(\cdot)\in\mathscr{U}[t,T]
such that

J(t,\xi;\bar{u}(\cdot))=\mathop{\rm essinf}_{u(\cdot)\in\mathscr{U}[t,T]}J(t,\xi;u(\cdot))=V(t,\xi).

(1.9)

Any
\bar{u}(\cdot)\in\mathscr{U}[t,T]
satisfying (
1.9
) is called an (
open-loop
)
optimal control
of Problem (N) for the initial pair
(t,\xi)
;
the corresponding state process
(\bar{X}(\cdot),\bar{Y}(\cdot),\bar{Z}(\cdot))\equiv\big(X(\cdot\,;t,\xi,u(\cdot)),Y(\cdot\,;t,\xi,u(\cdot)),Z(\cdot\,;t,\xi,u(\cdot))\big)

is called an (
open-loop
)
optimal state process
; and
V(\cdot\,,\cdot):\mathscr{D}\to\mathbb{R}
is called the
value function
of Problem (N).

We now briefly illustrate the major
motivation
of the above framework as follows:
The (vector-valued) process
X(\cdot)
follows a (forward) stochastic differential equation (FSDE, for short).
Components of
X(\cdot)
consist of two types processes: uncontrolled ones (by the individuals),
including prices of securities (such as bonds, stocks), some economic factors (such as interest rates, unemployment rates, GDP, etc.), and controlled ones (by the individuals), including market values of the investor’s wealth (subject to trading strategies),
inventory of commodities (subject to the ordering), amounts of goods (subject to the production), etc.
On the other hand, the components of
Y(\cdot)
, following a multi-dimensional backward stochastic differential equation
(BSDE, for short), could include the prices of some European type contingent claims of the underlying assets
(whose prices are some components of
X(\cdot)
), and some dynamic risk measures, and so on.
Therefore, it is natural to have an FBSDE as a state equation. Further, the dynamic expected utility/disutility of the total assets will be calculated in the recursive way, which can be described by the adapted solution to a BSVIE (see below).
Putting all the above together, we have the framework and the formulation of the problem.

Let us now briefly illustrate the recursive cost functional of form (
1.2
)–(
1.3
).
In 1992, Duffie–Epstein
[
17
,
18
]
introduced a stochastic differential formulation
of recursive utility in the case of information generated by a Brownian motion. In 1997, El Karoui–Peng–Quenez
[
20
]

showed that such a process actually is a part of the adapted solution to a particular BSDE and then they defined a more general class of recursive utilities,
through a general BSDE (see also Lazrak–Quenez
[
38
]
and Lazrak
[
37
]
for further developments).
The main feature of such a recursive process, denoted by
Y^{R}(\cdot)
, is that the current value
Y^{R}(t)
depends on the future values
Y^{R}(s)
,

t<s\leqslant T
of the process. Then, on top of the classical Bolza type cost functional (
1.6
), mimicking
[
20
]
,
for our FBSDE state equation, it is natural to introduce the following
recursive
cost functional:

J^{R}(t,\xi;u(\cdot))=Y^{R}(t),

(1.10)

where
Y^{R}(\cdot)
is determined by the following equation:

Y^{R}(r)=\mathbb{E}_{r}\Big[h^{0}(X(T),Y(r))+\int_{r}^{T}g^{0}(s,X(s),u(s),Y(s),Z(s),Y^{R}(s))ds\Big],\qquad r\in[t,T].

(1.11)

From the above, we see that the value
Y^{R}(t)
depends on the values
Y^{R}(s)
for
s\in[t,T]
,
through equation (
1.11
). Hence the cost process
Y^{R}(\cdot)
has a recursive feature, and thus its name.
By Yong
[
73
]
, for some process
Z^{R}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
,
the pair
(Y^{R}(\cdot),Z^{R}(\cdot\,,\cdot))
is the adapted solution to the following BSVIE:

Y^{R}(r)=h^{0}(X(T),Y(r))+\int_{r}^{T}g^{0}(s,X(s),u(s),Y(s),Z(s),Y^{R}(s))ds-\int_{r}^{T}Z^{R}(r,s)dW(s),\quad r\in[t,T].

(1.12)

Note that (
1.12
) (i.e., (
1.11
)) is not a BSDEs on
[t,T]
,
because the free term
h^{0}(X(T),Y(r))
depends on the time variable
r
,
which leads to the adjustment process
Z^{R}(r,s)
depending on
s
and
r
.
Inspired by the above, we introduce the general recursive cost functional (
1.2
)–(
1.3
).
Note that in BSVIE (
1.3
), the
free term

h^{0}(\cdot)
and the
generator

g^{0}(\cdot)

are allowed to depend on the initial pair
(r,X(r))
at the current time
r
,
which is motivated by the
non-exponential discounting

[
35
,
22
,
75
]

and the
state-dependent risk aversion

[
9
,
30
]
in finance.
The recursive cost functional of form (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) was introduced the first time
by Wang–Yong
[
66
]
, motivated by the recursive utility/disutility process for classical optimal control problems.
Comparing with the cost functional studied in
[
66
]
, we see that the free term
h^{0}(\cdot)

and the generator
g^{0}(\cdot)
of BSVIE (
1.3
) are additionally allowed to depend on the initial state
X(r)
and the backward process
(Y(\cdot),Z(\cdot))
.
Moreover, we highlight that (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) can also be regarded as a recursive version of the cost functional studied in Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
, because
\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[X(T)]
is the backward state process
Y(\cdot)
of a trivial BSDE.

It is well-known by now that the introduction of BSDEs by Bismut
[
5
,
6
]

in the early 1970s was for the purpose of studying optimal control of FSDEs.
The later developments of general BSDEs by Pardoux–Peng
[
48
]

(see also Duffie–Epstein
[
17
]
and El Karoui–Peng–Quenez
[
20
]
),
and the extension to FBSDEs by Antonelli
[
1
]
, Ma–Protter–Yong
[
42
]
,
Hu–Peng
[
31
]
(see also the books of Ma–Yong
[
43
]
and Zhang
[
80
]
)
have been attracting many researchers’ attention.
Among many other publications, a big number of literature on the optimal control problems for FBSDEs/BSDEs keep appearing.
See, Peng
[
50
]
, Xu
[
71
]
, Dokuchaev–Zhou
[
16
]
, Ji–Zhou
[
34
]
,
Shi–Wu
[
55
]
, Huang–Wang–Xiong
[
33
]
, Yong
[
74
]
,
Wang–Wu–Xiong
[
62
]
, and Hu–Ji–Xue
[
27
]
on the Pontryagin’s maximum principle
for controlled BSDEs/FBSDEs; Lim–Zhou
[
41
]
, Wang–Wu–Xiong
[
63
]
,
Huang–Wang–Wu
[
32
]
, Wang–Xiao–Xiong
[
64
]
, Li–Sun–Xiong
[
40
]
,
Hu–Ji–Xue
[
28
]
, Sun–Wang
[
58
]
, Sun–Wu–Xiong
[
61
]
, Sun–Wang–Wen
[
59
]
on the linear-quadratic (LQ, for short) optimal control problems for BSDEs/FBSDEs; and so on. It is observed that the problems investigated in the above listed works are all essentially the special cases of Problem (N),
and have been treated as usual stochastic optimal control problems.
There is an
essential feature
has been overlooked in all the above, which we now indicate that.

For a dynamic optimal control problem, suppose that at a given initial pair
(t,\xi)\in\mathscr{D}
,
the problem has an (open-loop) optimal control
\bar{u}(\cdot)\equiv\bar{u}(\cdot\,;t,\xi)

with the (open-loop) optimal state being
\bar{X}(\cdot)\equiv X(\cdot\,;t,\xi,\bar{u}(\cdot))
.
Then, we could not expect the following:

J\big(\tau,\bar{X}(\tau);\bar{u}(\cdot)\big|_{[\tau,T]}\big)=\inf_{u(\cdot)\in\mathscr{U}[\tau,T]}J(\tau,\bar{X}(\cdot);u(\cdot)),\qquad\forall\tau\in(t,T],~\hbox{a.s.}

(1.13)

In other words, an optimal control selected at a given initial pair might not stay optimal thereafter. Then, we say that the optimal control problem is
time-inconsistent
.
It turns out that, in general, Problem (N) is time-inconsistent,
as the
dynamical programming principle
(DPP, for short) does not hold.
This reveals a surprising feature of Problem (N). To see that, let us elaborate the time-inconsistency
in a little more details, from which we will see how Problem (N) is generally time-inconsistent.

\bullet

Time-preferences and discounting.

Suppose the continuously compound interest rate is a constant
\lambda>0
.
Then one needs to deposit an amount
e^{-\lambda T_{0}}
at
\tau
in order to get 1 unit at
\tau+T_{0}
.
We call
e^{-\lambda T_{0}}
the
discount factor
of the time interval
[\tau,\tau+T_{0}]
,
which could also be defined as the
value
of this time interval.
Clearly, such a value
e^{-\lambda T_{0}}
of
[\tau,\tau+T_{0}]
is independent of the initial time
\tau

and it is also independent of the time
t\in[0,\infty)
at which
[\tau,\tau+T_{0}]
is evaluated, either
t\leqslant\tau
or
t>\tau
.
Because of this, such an exponential evaluation is said to be
rational
. Or equivalently,
rationality can be described by the
exponential discounting
. On the other hand,
it is common that most people overweight the utility of the immediate future events,
which can be convinced by the fact that one often regrets the (optimal) decisions made earlier.
This means that people evaluate the immediate future time period more expensively than it should be,
which amounts to saying that the discount factor for that time interval is larger than the rational one.
Hence, we need to replace the exponential discounting by more general ones to more precisely describe the real situations.

In the above recursive cost functional (
1.2
)–(
1.3
), if we have

h^{0}(r,\tilde{x},x,y)=e^{-\lambda(T-r)}h^{0}(x),\qquad g^{0}(r,s,\tilde{x},x,u,y,z,y^{0},z^{0})=e^{-\lambda(s-r)}g^{0}(s,x,u),

for some discount rate
\lambda>0
, then the cost functional is reduced to the classical exponential discounting Bolza cost functional:

J(t,\xi;u(\cdot))=\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[e^{-\lambda(T-t)}h^{0}(X(T))+\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\lambda(s-t)}g^{0}(s,X(s),u(s))ds\Big].

In this case, there are no (European type) contingent claims involved, and there are no dynamic risks taken into account. Therefore, the BSDE for
(Y(\cdot),Z(\cdot))
in (
1.1
) is irrelevant. Also, the involved individual is completely
rational
(as far as the time-preferences are concerned). For such a case, the corresponding Problem (N) is time-consistent.
Now, if
e^{-\lambda(T-t)}
and
e^{-\lambda(s-t)}
are replaced by some non-exponential decay functions,
the cost functionals are referred to as non-exponential ones, which describe some kinds of
irrationality
of time-preferences for the involved individuals. In this case, namely, the cost functional is given by (
1.2
)–(
1.3
), our Problem (N) is time-inconsistent. The earliest mathematical consideration in this aspect was given by Strotz
[
57
]
, followed by Pollak
[
53
]
, and the recent works of Ekeland–Pirvu
[
22
]
, Ekeland–Lazrak
[
21
]
, Yong
[
75
,
77
,
78
]
, Wei–Yong–Yu
[
70
]
,
Mei–Yong
[
46
]
, Mei–Zhu
[
47
]
, Wang–Yong
[
66
]
, Hamaguchi
[
24
]
, and Hernández–Possamai
[
26
]
for various kinds of problems relevant to non-exponential discounting.

\bullet

Risk-preferences and nonlinear appearance of conditional expectations of the (terminal) state.

Different groups of people should have different opinions of risks on the in-coming events. This is referred to as people’s
subjective
risk-preferences. One way to describe this is to allow the conditional expectation of the state to (nonlinearly) appear in the cost functional. It turns out that such a formulation will lead to time-inconsistency of the optimal control problem in general.
See Basak–Chabakauri
[
3
]
, Hu–Jin–Zhou
[
29
,
30
]
,
Björk–Murgoci
[
8
]
, Björk–Murgoci–Zhou
[
9
]
,
Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
, Yong
[
78
]
,
and He–Jiang
[
25
]
for some relevant results.

Let us now make an interesting observation for our Problem (N). Let
m=n
, and

h(x)=x,\quad g(s,x,u,y,z)\equiv 0,\quad h^{0}(r,\tilde{x},x,y)=h^{0}(x,y),\quad g^{0}(r,s,\tilde{x},x,u,y,z,y^{0},z^{0})=g^{0}(s,x,u,y),

then

Y(s)=\mathbb{E}_{s}[X(T)],\qquad s\in[t,T],

and the recursive cost functional (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) becomes

J(t,\xi;u(\cdot))=\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[h^{0}\big(X(T),\mathbb{E}_{t}[X(T)]\big)+\int_{t}^{T}g^{0}\big(s,X(s),u(s),\mathbb{E}_{s}[X(T)]\big)ds\Big].

In the above,
\mathbb{E}_{t}[X(T)]
appears nonlinearly and the corresponding optimal control problem is time-inconsistent. From the above observation, we see that the state equation being an FBSDE can include many situations of nonlinear appearance of conditional expectations. Therefore, Problem (N) is intrinsically time-inconsistent. Some special cases were investigated by Basak–Chabakauri
[
3
]
, Hu–Jin–Zhou
[
29
,
30
]
, Björk–Murgoci–Zhou
[
9
]
, Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
.
Our Problem (N) also partially covers the case studied in Yong
[
78
]
.

We have seen that Problem (N) is generally time-inconsistent.
Therefore, we should treat it from the angle differently from the usual classical ones.
Before going further, let us present the following simple example,
from which we will see further the essential reason for Problem (N) to be time-inconsistent.

Example 1.1
.

Consider the one-dimensional (degenerate) FBSDE state equation

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{X}(s)=0,\qquad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\dot{Y}(s)=u(s),\qquad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle X(t)=x,\qquad Y(T)=0,\end{array}\right.

(1.14)

with the cost functional

J(t,x;u(\cdot))=\int_{t}^{T}[Y(s)+u(s)+|u(s)|^{2}]ds.

(1.15)

A straightforward calculation (see
3.2
for details) shows that at the initial pair
(t,x)
,
the unique optimal control
\bar{u}(\cdot\,;t,x)
is given by

\bar{u}(s)\equiv\bar{u}(s;t,x)={s-t-1\over 2},\qquad s\in[t,T].

Then, for any
\tau\in(t,T)
, the unique optimal control at
(\tau,\bar{X}(\tau))\equiv(\tau,x)
is given by

\tilde{u}(s)\equiv\tilde{u}(s;\tau,\bar{X}(\tau))={s-\tau-1\over 2},\qquad s\in[\tau,T].

Clearly,

\bar{u}(s)\neq\tilde{u}(s),\qquad s\in[\tau,T].

Thus, the problem is time-inconsistent.

It is worthy of pointing out that in the above example, (
1.15
) is a Bolza type cost functional for FBSDE state equations, and unlike Yong
[
75
]
and Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
, neither non-exponential discounting
nor conditional expectations (nonlinearly) appear. Furthermore, the controlled system (
1.14
) is a deterministic ordinary differential equation, and the terminal cost of (
1.15
) equals zero,
due to which (
1.15
) is also a Lagrange type cost functional.
This tells us that
an optimal control problem could be time-inconsistent solely
because the state equation is a forward-backward one
.
Hence, the time-inconsistency feature is intrinsically contained in the optimal control problems for FBSDEs. Such a feature distinguishes the current paper from the previous ones concerning the time-inconsistency, in other aspects.

Having the above time-inconsistent feature of the problem, we now highlight the main results of this paper.

(i)

Using Pontryagin’s maximum principle, we will show that Problem (N) is generically time-inconsistent.
The advantage of such an approach is that we are not satisfied with just some counterexamples,
instead, we will show that if
\bar{u}(\cdot)
is optimal at
(t,\xi)
, which will satisfy the Pontragin’s type maximum principle (MP, for short) on
[t,T]
, the
\bar{u}(\cdot)\big|_{[t,T]}
hardly satisfies MP on
[\tau,T]
. Therefore, (
1.13
) should not be expected in general.

(ii)

Since Problem (N) is time-inconsistent in general,
finding an optimal control at any given initial pair
(t,\xi)
is not very useful.
Instead, one should find an
equilibrium strategy
, which is time-consistent and possesses certain kind of local optimality. Inspired by Yong
[
75
]
, we derive the
equilibrium HJB equation
associated with Problem (N),
through which an equilibrium strategy can be constructed. Our equilibrium HJB equation can cover the results obtained in
Yong
[
75
]
and Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
.
In the case that the recursive cost functional is governed by a BSDE, one could apply the method of multi-person differential games,
by viewing that the controller is playing a cooperative game with all his incarnations in the future.
Such an idea can be traced back to the work of Pollak
[
53
]
in 1968.
Later, the approach was adopted and further developed in
[
21
,
22
,
75
,
77
,
78
,
8
,
9
,
7
,
70
,
46
,
47
,
66
]
.
We point out that the multi-person differential game approach used in
[
75
,
70
]

does not directly apply to Problem (N) of the current paper, because the DPP does not hold
for controlled FBSDEs even if the cost functional does not depend on the initial values
(t,X(t),Y(t))
.
We overcome the difficulty by making use of the Feymann–Kac formula for BSVIEs,
which has been recently well-developed in our works
[
69
,
65
,
67
]
.
In the proof of the verification theorem, some technical assumptions imposed in

[
70
,
66
]
and
[
7
]
are relaxed.

(iii)

When the diffusion term of the forward state equation does not depend on the control
u(\cdot)
,
the equilibrium HJB equation associated with Problem (N) is a system of semi-linear parabolic partial differential equations
with non-local terms. Under the non-degenerate condition, the well-posdness of the equilibrium HJB equation is established
in the sense of classical solutions.

(iv)

Some comparisons between our equilibrium HJB equations and those derived by Peng
[
52
]
,
by Yong
[
75
,
77
]
, and by Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
are carefully made, respectively. We find that the backward controlled equation has a significant influence on the form of the
associated equilibrium HJB equation.
When Problem (N) is reduced to the problem studied by Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
,
the form of our equilibrium HJB equations is more natural than their so-called
extended HJB equation
.
We note that there was no rigorous proof on the well-posedness of the extended HJB equation presented in
[
7
]
.
In addition to the above, the “HJB equation” associated with the value function of Problem (N) is formally derived,
provided Problem (N) has an optimal control with the closed-loop representation,
which can be regarded as a PDE approach version of Peng
[
50
]
and Yong
[
74
]
.
By comparing the equilibrium HJB equation and the “HJB equation”, we find that the optimality condition
of the “HJB equation” is not a minimization problem in the finite
dimensional space, due to which the “HJB equation” is not useful and Problem (N) is time-inconsistent in general.

(v)

The linear-quadratic optimal control problems for FBSDEs are briefly studied
and a linear equilibrium strategy is obtained, provided the associated Riccati equation is solvable. This partially covers the work of Yong
[
78
]
. Further, as applications, a mean-variance model, a social planner model of Merton’s consumption–portfolio selection with heterogeneous Epstein–Zin utilities, and a Stackelberg game are investigated, which are all special cases of Problem (N). It is shown that these specific problems are all time-inconsistent, and by the theoretical results obtained in the paper, the associated equilibrium strategies can be explicitly constructed.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we state the main results of our paper, with some explanations.
In Section
3
, we compare the results obtained in the paper with the existing ones.
The linear-quadratic problem is studied in Section
4
,
and three applications are presented in Section
5
.
In Section
6
, the verification theorem is proved.
Some technical and lengthy proofs are given in Section
7
.

2
The Main Results

2.1
Preliminaries: Notations and Feynman–Kac formula

Let
T>0
be a given time horizon and recall the upper triangle domain
\Delta^{*}[0,T]
from (
1.4
). Let
\mathbb{S}^{n}
be the subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
consisting of symmetric matrices and

U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{l}
be a nonempty measurable set which could be bounded or unbounded.
We will use
K>0
to represent a generic constant which could be different from line to line. For any Euclidean space
\mathbb{H}
(as well as
\mathbb{H}_{1}
,
\mathbb{H}_{2}
),
we introduce the following spaces:

\displaystyle L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathbb{H}))=\Big\{\varphi:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{H}\bigm|\varphi(\cdot)~\hbox{is $\mathbb{F}$-adapted, pathwise continuous, }\mathbb{E}\big[\displaystyle\sup_{0\leqslant s\leqslant T}|\varphi(s)|^{2}\big]<\infty\Big\};

\displaystyle C([0,T];L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{H}))=\Big\{\varphi:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{H}\bigm|\varphi(\cdot)~\hbox{is $\mathbb{F}$-adapted, $\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\cdot)]$ is continuous, }\displaystyle\sup_{0\leqslant s\leqslant T}\mathbb{E}\big[|\varphi(s)|^{2}\big]<\infty\Big\};

\displaystyle L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})=\Big\{\varphi:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{H}\bigm|\varphi(\cdot)~\hbox{is $\mathbb{F}$-progressively measurable on $[0,T]$, }\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}|\varphi(s)|^{2}ds<\infty\Big\};

\displaystyle C([0,T];L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(\cdot\,,T;\mathbb{H}))\negthinspace=\negthinspace\Big\{\varphi\negthinspace:\negthinspace\Delta^{*}[0,T]\negthinspace\times\negthinspace\Omega\negthinspace\to\negthinspace\mathbb{H}\bigm|\varphi(t,\cdot)\negthinspace\in\negthinspace L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(t,T;\mathbb{H}),~t\negthinspace\in\negthinspace[0,T],~\mathbb{E}\negthinspace\int_{\cdot}^{T}\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace|\varphi(\cdot\,,s)|^{2}ds\negthinspace\in\negthinspace C([0,T])\Big\};

\displaystyle L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}_{1};\mathbb{H}_{2})=\big\{\varphi:\mathbb{H}_{1}\to\mathbb{H}_{2}\bigm|\varphi(\cdot)~\hbox{is essentially bounded}\big\};

\displaystyle C^{k}(\mathbb{H}_{1};\mathbb{H}_{2})=\big\{\varphi:\mathbb{H}_{1}\to\mathbb{H}_{2}\bigm|\varphi(\cdot)~\hbox{is $j$-th continuously differentiable for any $0\leqslant j\leqslant k$}\big\};

\displaystyle C_{b}^{k}(\mathbb{H}_{1};\mathbb{H}_{2})=\big\{\varphi:\mathbb{H}_{1}\to\mathbb{H}_{2}\bigm|\varphi(\cdot)\in C^{k}(\mathbb{H}_{1};\mathbb{H}_{2}),~\hbox{the $j$-th derivatives are bounded, $0\leqslant j\leqslant k$}\big\}.

To guarantee the well-posedness of the controlled FBSDE (
1.1
) and BSVIE (
1.3
) governing the recursive cost functional, we introduce the following assumptions.

(H1)
.

Let the mappings
b,\sigma:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

g:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
and
h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be continuous. There exists a constant
L>0
such that

\displaystyle|b(s,0,u)|+|\sigma(s,0,u)|+|h(0)|+|g(s,0,u,0,0)|\leqslant L(1+|u|),

\displaystyle|b(s,x_{1},u)-b(s,x_{2},u)|+|\sigma(s,x_{1},u)-\sigma(s,x_{2},u)|+|h(x_{1})-h(x_{2})|

\displaystyle+|g(s,x_{1},u,y_{1},z_{1})-g(s,x_{2},u,y_{2},z_{2})|\leqslant L\big[|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|y_{1}-y_{2}|+|z_{1}-z_{2}|\big],

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\forall(s,u)\in[0,T]\times U,~(x_{i},y_{i},z_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},~i=1,2.

(H2)
.

Let the mappings
h^{0}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}

and
g^{0}:\Delta^{*}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous. There exists a constant
L>0
such that

\displaystyle|h^{0}(t,0,0,0)|+|g^{0}(t,s,0,0,u,0,0,0,0)|\leqslant L(1+|u|),

\displaystyle|g^{0}(t_{1},s,\tilde{x}_{1},x_{1},u,y_{1},z_{1},y^{0}_{1},z^{0}_{1})-g^{0}(t_{2},s,\tilde{x}_{2},x_{2},u,y_{2},z_{2},y^{0}_{2},z^{0}_{2})|

\displaystyle+|h^{0}(t_{1},\tilde{x}_{1},x_{1},y_{1})-h^{0}(t_{2},\tilde{x}_{2},x_{2},y_{2})|

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant L\big[|t_{1}-t_{2}|+|\tilde{x}_{1}-\tilde{x}_{2}|+|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|y_{1}-y_{2}|+|z_{1}-z_{2}|+|y^{0}_{1}-y^{0}_{2}|+|z^{0}_{1}-z^{0}_{2}|\big],

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall(t_{i},s)\in\Delta^{*}[0,T],~\tilde{x}_{i},x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},~u\in U,~y_{i},z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},~y^{0}_{i},z^{0}_{i}\in\mathbb{R},~i=1,2.

By Yong–Zhou
[
79
, Chapter 7]
and Yong
[
73
]
,
we have the following results about the well-posedness of (decoupled) FBSDE (
1.1
) and BSVIE (
1.3
).

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
(H1)
hold. Then for any initial pair
(t,\xi)\in\mathscr{D}
and control
u(\cdot)\in\mathscr{U}[t,T]
,
state equation (
1.1
) admits a unique adapted solution
(X(\cdot),Y(\cdot),Z(\cdot))\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(\Omega;C([t,T];\mathbb{R}^{n}))\times L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(\Omega;C([t,T];\mathbb{R}^{m}))\times L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(t,T;\mathbb{R}^{m})
. Moreover, there exists a constant
K>0
, independent of
(t,\xi)
and
u(\cdot)
, such that

\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[\sup_{t\leqslant r\leqslant T}\big[|X(r)|^{2}+|Y(r)|^{2}\big]+\int_{t}^{T}|Z(s)|^{2}ds\Big]\leqslant K\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[1+|\xi|^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}|u(s)|^{2}ds\Big].

(2.1)

In addition, if
(H2)
also holds, then for any initial pair
(t,\xi)\in\mathscr{D}
, control
u(\cdot)\in\mathscr{U}[t,T]
,
and the corresponding state process
(X(\cdot),Y(\cdot),Z(\cdot))
, BSVIE (
1.3
) admits a unique adapted solution

(Y^{0}(\cdot),Z^{0}(\cdot\,,\cdot))\in C([t,T];L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}))\times C([t,T];L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}(\cdot\,,T;\mathbb{R}))
.
Moreover, there exists a constant
K>0
, independent of
(t,\xi)
and
u(\cdot)
, such that

\sup_{t\leqslant r\leqslant T}\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[|Y^{0}(r)|^{2}+\int_{r}^{T}|Z^{0}(r,s)|^{2}ds\Big]\leqslant K\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[1+|\xi|^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}|u(s)|^{2}ds\Big].

(2.2)

As another preparation, we consider the following system of FBSDEs and BSVIEs without controls:

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle X(r)=\xi+\int_{t}^{r}b(s,X(s))ds+\int_{t}^{r}\sigma(s,X(s))dW(s),\quad r\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle Y(r)=h(X(T))+\int_{r}^{T}g(s,X(s),Y(s),Z(s))ds-\int_{r}^{T}Z(s)dW(s),\quad r\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle Y^{0}(r)=h^{0}(r,X(r),X(T),Y(r))+\int_{r}^{T}g^{0}(r,s,X(r),X(s),Y(s),Z(s),Y^{0}(s),Z^{0}(r,s))ds\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\qquad\quad\,~-\int_{r}^{T}Z^{0}(r,s)dW(s),\qquad r\in[t,T],\end{array}\right.

(2.3)

where the coefficients
b(\cdot),\sigma(\cdot),h(\cdot),g(\cdot),h^{0}(\cdot),g^{0}(\cdot)
satisfy
(H1)
–
(H2)

(independent of the control
u
). Suggested by Wang–Yong
[
69
]

and Wang–Yong–Zhang
[
67
]
,
we introduce the following system of semi-linear PDEs:

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\Theta_{s}^{k}(s,x)+{1\over 2}\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}^{k}(s,x)\sigma(s,x)\sigma(s,x)^{\top}]+\Theta^{k}_{x}(s,x)b(s,x)\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad+g\big(s,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x)\big)=0,\quad(s,x)\in[t,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad 1\leqslant k\leqslant m,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta^{0}_{s}(r,s,\tilde{x},x,y)+{1\over 2}\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta^{0}_{xx}(r,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\sigma(s,x)\sigma(s,x)^{\top}]+\Theta^{0}_{x}(r,s,\tilde{x},x,y)b(s,x)\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad+g^{0}\big(r,s,\tilde{x},x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(r,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\sigma(s,x)\big)=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad(r,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\in\Delta^{*}[t,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta(T,x)=h(x),\quad\Theta^{0}(r,T,\tilde{x},x,y)=h^{0}(r,\tilde{x},x,y),\qquad(r,\tilde{x},x,y)\in[t,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\end{array}\right.

(2.4)

with
\Theta=(\Theta^{1},\cdots,\Theta^{m})^{\top}
. Note that
\Theta^{0}(\cdot)
is a function of
(r,s,\tilde{x},x,y)
,
and
\Theta^{0}_{x}(\cdot),\Theta^{0}_{xx}(\cdot)
are the derivatives with respect to the 4th argument.
We have the following representation theorem.

Proposition 2.2
.

Suppose that PDE (
2.4
) admits a classical solution
(\Theta(\cdot\,,\cdot),\Theta^{0}(\cdot\,,\cdot\,,\cdot\,,\cdot\,,\cdot))
.
Assume that the system (
2.3
) of FBSDEs and BSVIEs admits a unique adapted solution
(X(\cdot),Y(\cdot),Z(\cdot),Y^{0}(\cdot),Z^{0}(\cdot,\cdot))
.
Then the following representation holds:

\displaystyle Y(r)=\Theta(r,X(r)),\quad Z(r)=\Theta_{x}(r,X(r))\sigma(r,X(r)),\qquad r\in[t,T],\,\hbox{a.s.},

\displaystyle Y^{0}(r)=\Theta^{0}\big(r,r,X(r),X(r),\Theta(r,X(r))\big),\qquad r\in[t,T],\,\hbox{a.s.}

\displaystyle Z^{0}(r,s)=\Theta^{0}_{x}\big(r,s,X(r),X(s),\Theta(r,X(r))\big)\sigma(s,X(s)),\quad(r,s)\in\Delta^{*}[t,T],\,\hbox{a.s.}

(2.5)

Proof.

By the Feynman–Kac formula for BSDEs (see Pardoux–Peng
[
49
]
, for example), we get

Y(r)=\Theta(r,X(r)),\quad Z(r)=\Theta_{x}(r,X(r))\sigma(r,X(r)),\quad r\in[t,T].

Substituting the above into the BSVIE in (
2.3
), we get

\displaystyle Y^{0}(r)

\displaystyle=h^{0}(r,X(r),X(T),\Theta(r,X(r)))-\int_{r}^{T}Z^{0}(r,s)dW(s)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{r}^{T}g^{0}\big(r,s,X(r),X(s),\Theta(s,X(s)),\Theta_{x}(s,X(s))\sigma(s,X(s)),Y^{0}(s),Z^{0}(r,s)\big)ds,\quad r\in[t,T].

Then by the Feynman–Kac formula for BSVIEs (see
[
69
,
65
]
), we have

\displaystyle Y^{0}(r)=\widehat{\Theta}^{0}\big(r,r,X(r),X(r)\big),\qquad r\in[t,T],\,\hbox{a.s.},

\displaystyle Z^{0}(r,s)=\widehat{\Theta}^{0}_{x}\big(r,s,X(r),X(s))\big)\sigma(s,X(s)),\quad(r,s)\in\Delta^{*}[t,T],\,\hbox{a.s.}

with
\widehat{\Theta}^{0}(\cdot\,,\cdot\,,\cdot\,,\cdot)
being the classical solution to the following PDE:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\widehat{\Theta}^{0}_{s}(r,s,\tilde{x},x)+{1\over 2}\hbox{tr$\,$}[\widehat{\Theta}^{0}_{xx}(r,s,\tilde{x},x)\sigma(s,x)\sigma(s,x)^{\top}]+\widehat{\Theta}^{0}_{x}(r,s,\tilde{x},x)b(s,x)\\
&\quad+g^{0}\big(r,s,\tilde{x},x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x),\widehat{\Theta}^{0}(s,s,x,x),\widehat{\Theta}^{0}_{x}(r,s,\tilde{x},x)\sigma(s,x)\big)=0,\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad(r,s,\tilde{x},x)\in\Delta^{*}[t,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
&\widehat{\Theta}^{0}(r,T,\tilde{x},x)=h^{0}(r,\tilde{x},x,\Theta(r,\tilde{x})),\qquad(r,\tilde{x},x)\in[t,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{aligned}\right.

Clearly,
\widehat{\Theta}^{0}(\cdot\,,\cdot\,,\cdot\,,\cdot)
is actually given by

\widehat{\Theta}^{0}(r,s,\tilde{x},x)=\Theta^{0}(r,s,\tilde{x},x,\Theta(r,\tilde{x})),\quad(r,s,\tilde{x},x)\in\Delta^{*}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Then the desired results can be obtained immediately.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

2.2
is a generalization of the representation/Feynman–Kac formula for Markovian BSVIEs,
which was established by Wang–Yong
[
69
]
and Wang
[
65
]
, in the sense of classical solutions.
Under the non-degenerate assumption, the well-posedness of PDE (
2.4
) will be established by an analytic method, as a byproduct of
Theorem 2.10
. The probabilistic approach, without the non-degenerate assumption,
can be also obtained by the arguments in Wang–Yong–Zhang
[
67
]
.

2.2
Time-Inconsistency Analysis of Problem (N)

In this subsection, we shall discuss the time-inconsistency of Problem (N) from the Pontryagin’s maximum principle viewpoint.
For simplicity, we consider the case that (
1.5
) holds so that the cost functional reads as (
1.6
)
(of Bolza type, without involving BSVIEs). Also, we suppose that the control domain
U\equiv\mathbb{R}^{l}
and all involved functions are continuously differentiable. Let
\left(\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\cdot),\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(\cdot),\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(\cdot),\bar{Z}^{t,\xi}(\cdot)\right)
be an optimal 4-tuple (supposing it exists) of Problem (N) on
[t,T]
with a given initial pair
(t,\xi)\in\mathscr{D}
, for which we assume to be time-consistent. Then, for any
\tau\in(t,T]
,

J(\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau);\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(\cdot){\bf 1}_{[\tau,T]}(\cdot))=\inf_{u(\cdot)\in\mathscr{U}[\tau,T]}J(\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau);u(\cdot)),

(2.6)

and

\displaystyle\big(\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(s),\,\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(s),\,\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(s),\,\bar{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\quad=\big(\bar{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s),\,\bar{u}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s),\,\bar{Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s),\,\bar{Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)\big),\quad s\in[\tau,T],~\hbox{a.s.}

(2.7)

Now, we denote

\displaystyle\bar{b}^{t,\xi}_{x}(s)=b_{x}(s,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(s)),\quad

\displaystyle\bar{b}_{u}^{t,\xi}(s)=b_{u}(s,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(s)),

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{x}(s)=g_{x}(s,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)),\quad

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)=g_{u}(s,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)),

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{y}(s)=g_{y}(s,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)),\quad

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{z}(s)=g_{z}(s,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(s),\bar{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)),

\displaystyle\bar{h}^{t,\xi}_{x}(T)=h_{x}(\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(T)),\quad

\displaystyle\bar{h}^{0,t,\xi}_{x}(t)=h_{x}(\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(T),\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(t)),

and
\bar{\sigma}^{t,\xi}_{x}(s)
,
\bar{\sigma}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)
,
\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{x}(s)
,
\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{u}(s)
,
\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{y}(s)
,
\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{z}(s)
,
\bar{h}^{0,t,\xi}_{y}(t)
are defined similarly.
Then by applying the Pontryagin’s maximum principle (see
[
50
,
27
,
28
]
, for examples), to the optimal 4-tuple on
[t,T]
and
[\tau,T]
, respectively,
we get the following
stationarity conditions
:

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}+\bar{g}_{u}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{b}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}{\cal Y}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{\sigma}_{u}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal Z}^{t,\xi}(s)=0,\qquad s\in[t,T],

(2.8)

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}+\bar{g}_{u}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)+\bar{b}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}{\cal Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)+\bar{\sigma}_{u}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)=0,\qquad s\in[\tau,T],

(2.9)

where
({\cal Y}^{t,\xi}(\cdot),{\cal Z}^{t,\xi}(\cdot))
is the co-state process pair of
\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\cdot)
, and
{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(\cdot)

is the co-state process of
(\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(\cdot)
,
\bar{Z}^{t,\xi}(\cdot))
, for which the following holds on
[t,T]
almost surely:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \displaystyle d{\cal Y}^{t,\xi}(s)&=-\big[\bar{g}_{x}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{b}_{x}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal Y}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{\sigma}_{x}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal Z}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{x}(s)^{\top}\big]ds+{\cal Z}^{t,\xi}(s)dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle d{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(s)&=\big[\bar{g}_{y}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{y}(s)^{\top}\big]ds+\big[\bar{g}_{z}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{z}(s)^{\top}\big]dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle{\cal Y}^{t,\xi}(T)&=\bar{h}_{x}^{t,\xi}(T)^{\top}{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(T)+\bar{h}^{0,t,\xi}_{x}(t)^{\top},\qquad{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(t)=\mathbb{E}_{t}[\bar{h}^{0,t,\xi}_{y}(t)^{\top}],\end{aligned}\right.

(2.10)

and
({\cal Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\cdot),{\cal Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\cdot))
is the co-state process pair of
\bar{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\cdot)
, and
{\cal X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\cdot)
is the co-state process of
(\bar{Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\cdot)
,
\bar{Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\cdot))
, for which the following holds on
[\tau,T]
almost surely:

\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{aligned} d{\cal Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)&=-\big[\bar{g}_{x}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)\negthinspace+\negthinspace\bar{b}_{x}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)\negthinspace+\negthinspace\bar{\sigma}_{x}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)\negthinspace+\negthinspace\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{x}(s)^{\top}\big]ds\\
&\quad+{\cal Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)dW(s),\\
d{\cal X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)&=\big[\bar{g}_{y}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)+\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{y}(s)^{\top}\big]ds+\big[\bar{g}_{z}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}{\cal X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)+\bar{g}^{0,t,\xi}_{z}(s)^{\top}\big]dW(s),\\
{\cal Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(T)&=\bar{h}_{x}^{t,\xi}(T)^{\top}{\cal X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(T)+\bar{h}^{0,t,\xi}_{x}(\tau)^{\top},\qquad{\cal X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\tau)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[\bar{h}^{0,t,\xi}_{y}(\tau)^{\top}].\end{aligned}\right.

(2.11)

We may state the conclusion as follows.

Proposition 2.4
.

If the optimal 4-tuple
(\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\cdot),\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(\cdot),\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(\cdot),\bar{Z}^{t,\xi}(\cdot))
is time-consistent, then (
2.9
) holds for any
\tau\in(t,T]
, subject to (
2.11
).

The necessary condition (
2.9
) with
\tau\in[t,T]

can be regarded as a
dynamic
version of the Pontryagin’s maximum principle.
Interestingly, we can use it to characterize the time-consistency of the optimal controls.

Now, let us make a careful comparison between (
2.10
) and (
2.11
).
First of all, these decoupled FBSDEs have exactly the same coefficients.
If we restrict (
2.10
) on
[\tau,T]
, then it has the initial condition
{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)
,
and we do not expect the following:

{\cal X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[\bar{h}^{0,t,\xi}_{y}(\tau)^{\top}]\equiv\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\big[h_{x}(\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(T),\bar{Y}^{t,\xi}(t))^{\top}\big],\qquad\tau\in[t,T].

(2.12)

Hence, in general, the following cannot be guaranteed:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\big({\cal X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s),\,\bar{u}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s),\,{\cal Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s),\,{\cal Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)\big)=\big({\cal X}^{t,\xi}(s),\,\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(s),\,{\cal Y}^{t,\xi}(s),\,{\cal Z}^{t,\xi}(s)\big),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad s\in[\tau,T],~\hbox{a.s.},~\forall\tau\in[t,T].\end{array}

Consequently, having (
2.8
), it is too much to request (
2.9
). From this, we see that Problem (N) is intrinsically time-inconsistent.

2.3
Equilibrium Strategy and Equilibrium HJB Equation

Since Problem (N) is time-inconsistent in general, we shall find the equilibrium strategy,
whose definition is given as follows.

Definition 2.5
.

A mapping
\Psi:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to U
is called a
feedback strategy
(of state equation (
1.1
)) on
[0,T]

if for every
(t,\xi)\in\mathscr{D}
, the following
closed-loop
system:

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle dX(s)=b(s,X(s),\Psi(s,X(s)))ds+\sigma(s,X(s),\Psi(s,X(s)))dW(s),\quad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle dY(s)=-g(s,X(s),\Psi(s,X(s)),Y(s),Z(s))ds+Z(s)dW(s),\quad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle X(t)=\xi,\qquad Y(T)=h(X(T)),\end{array}\right.

(2.13)

admits a unique adapted solution
(X(\cdot),Y(\cdot),Z(\cdot))\equiv\big(X(\cdot\,;t,\xi,\Psi(\cdot\,,\cdot)),Y(\cdot\,;t,\xi,\Psi(\cdot\,,\cdot)),Z(\cdot\,;t,\xi,\Psi(\cdot\,,\cdot))\big)\equiv(X^{\Psi}(\cdot)
,
Y^{\Psi}(\cdot),Z^{\Psi}(\cdot))

and the
outcome

u^{\Psi}(\cdot)\equiv\Psi(\cdot\,,X^{\Psi}(\cdot))
of
\Psi(\cdot\,,\cdot)
belongs to
\mathscr{U}[t,T]
.

We now introduce the following definition.

Definition 2.6
.

A feedback strategy
\bar{\Psi}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
, with
\bar{X}(\cdot)
being the forward component of the corresponding state process, is called an
equilibrium strategy
if

\mathop{\underline{\rm lim}}_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}{J(t,\bar{X}(t);\Psi^{\varepsilon}(\cdot))-J(t,\bar{X}(t);\bar{\Psi}(\cdot))\over\varepsilon}\geqslant 0,

(2.14)

for any
t\in[0,T)
and
u\in L_{{\cal F}_{t}}^{2}(\Omega;U)
, where

\Psi^{\varepsilon}(s)\triangleq\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}(s,X^{\varepsilon}(s)),&s\in[t+\varepsilon,T];\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle u,&s\in[t,t+\varepsilon),\end{array}\right.

(2.15)

with
X^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)\triangleq X^{\Psi^{\varepsilon}}(\cdot)\equiv X(\cdot\,;t,\bar{X}(t),\Psi^{\varepsilon}(\cdot\,,\cdot))
being the forward component of the state process corresponding to
\Psi^{\varepsilon}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
.

The intuition behind
2.6
is similar to that in
[
75
,
29
,
7
,
66
]
.
At any given time
t
, the controller is playing a game (cooperatively) with all his/her incarnations in the future by minimizing his/her cost functional on
[t,t+\varepsilon)
, and knowing that he/she will lose the control of the system beyond
t+\varepsilon
. We now briefly list our main results as follows.

To find an equilibrium strategy of Problem (N), we introduce the following spaces:

\displaystyle{\cal A}[0,T]:=[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},

\displaystyle{\cal A}^{0}[0,T]:=\Delta^{*}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}.

For simplicity, we denote

\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\theta,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},\qquad\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0}=(\theta^{0},p^{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}.

Now, we define the following Hamiltonians:

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}(s,x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},P)\negthinspace=\negthinspace\hbox{tr$\,$}[Pa(s,x,u)]\negthinspace+\negthinspace pb(s,x,u)\negthinspace+\negthinspace g\big(s,x,u,\theta,p\sigma(s,x,u)\big),

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},P^{0})\negthinspace=\negthinspace\hbox{tr$\,$}[P^{0}a(s,x,u)]\negthinspace+\negthinspace p^{0}b(s,x,u)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad~\negthinspace+\negthinspace g^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\theta,p\sigma(s,x,u),\theta^{0},p^{0}\sigma(s,x,u)),

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},P,\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0},P^{0})\negthinspace=\negthinspace{{\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},P^{0})}+q^{0}{\bm{H}}(s,x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},P),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0})\in{\cal A}^{0}[0,T],~P\in\big[\mathbb{S}^{n}\big]^{m},~q^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times m},~P^{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n},

(2.16)

where
a(\cdot)
is defined by

a(s,x,u)={1\over 2}\sigma(s,x,u)\sigma(s,x,u)^{\top},\quad(s,x,u)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U,

and, with
P=(P^{1},P^{2},\cdots,P^{m})^{\top}\in[\mathbb{S}^{n}]^{m}
,

\hbox{tr$\,$}\big[Pa(s,x,u)\big]=\begin{pmatrix}\hbox{tr$\,$}\big[P^{1}a(s,x,u)\big]\\
\hbox{tr$\,$}\big[P^{2}a(s,x,u)\big]\\
\vdots\\
\hbox{tr$\,$}\big[P^{m}a(s,x,u)\big]\end{pmatrix}.

In what follows, we will use the following hypothesis.

(H3)
.

Suppose that there exists a unique mapping
\psi(\cdot)
such that

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\psi(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\boldsymbol{\Theta},P,\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0},P^{0}),\boldsymbol{\Theta},P,\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0},P^{0})=\inf_{u\in U}\widehat{\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},P,\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0},P^{0}),

\displaystyle\forall(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0})\in{\cal A}^{0}[0,T],~P\in\big[\mathbb{S}^{n}\big]^{m},~q^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times m},~P^{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n}.

(2.17)

Moreover, we suppose that
\psi(\cdot)
is smooth enough with bounded derivatives.

We now introduce the following
equilibrium HJB equation
:

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\Theta_{s}(s,x)+{\bm{H}}\big(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x),\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta_{xx}(s,x)\big)=0,\quad(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta^{0}_{s}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\negthinspace+\negthinspace{\bm{H}}^{0}\big(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x),\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\qquad\Theta_{x}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y),\Theta^{0}_{xx}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\big)=0,\quad(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\in\Delta^{*}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta(T,x)=h(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta^{0}(t,T,\tilde{x},x,y)=h^{0}(t,\tilde{x},x,y),\quad(t,\tilde{x},x,y)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m},\end{array}\right.

(2.18)

where

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\psi\big(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta_{xx}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{xx}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))\big),\qquad(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(2.19)

We have the following result.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
\bar{\Psi}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to U
be defined by (
2.19
),
with
(\Theta(\cdot),\Theta^{0}(\cdot))
being the classical solution to the equilibrium HJB equation (
2.18
).
Let
\bar{\Psi}(\cdot,\cdot)
be a feedback strategy.
Then it is an equilibrium strategy of
Problem (N)
.

Remark 2.8
.

If the cost functional reads as (
1.6
), then we have

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}^{0}(s,x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},P^{0})\negthinspace=\negthinspace\hbox{tr$\,$}[P^{0}a(s,x,u)]\negthinspace+\negthinspace p^{0}b(s,x,u)\negthinspace+\negthinspace g^{0}(s,x,u,\theta,p\sigma(s,x,u)),

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{H}}^{0}(s,x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},P,\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0},P^{0})\negthinspace=\negthinspace\hbox{tr$\,$}[P^{0}a(s,x,u)]\negthinspace+\negthinspace p^{0}b(s,x,u)\negthinspace+\negthinspace g^{0}(s,x,u,\theta,p\sigma(s,x,u))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+q^{0}\Big(\hbox{tr$\,$}[Pa(s,u,x)]+pb(s,x,u)+g(s,x,u,\theta,p\sigma(s,x,u))\Big),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\forall(s,x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},P,\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0},P^{0})\in{\cal A}[0,T]\negthinspace\times\negthinspace[\mathbb{S}^{n}]^{m}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}\times\mathbb{R}^{1\times m}\times\mathbb{S}^{n}.

(2.20)

Remark 2.9
.

Theorem 2.7
is a verification theorem for Problem (N),
whose proof is given in Section
6
.
Taking the equilibrium strategy
\bar{\Psi}(\cdot,\cdot)
in (
1.1
) and (
1.3
),
we get the following equilibrium system on
[0,T]
:

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle d\bar{X}(s)=b(s,\bar{X}(s),\bar{\Psi}(s,\bar{X}(s)))ds+\sigma(s,\bar{X}(s),\bar{\Psi}(s,\bar{X}(s)))dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle d\bar{Y}(s)=-g(s,\bar{X}(s),\bar{\Psi}(s,\bar{X}(s)),\bar{Y}(s),\bar{Z}(s))ds+\bar{Z}(s)dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\bar{X}(0)=\xi,\qquad\bar{Y}(T)=h(\bar{X}(T)),\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle\bar{Y}^{0}(r)

\displaystyle=h^{0}(r,\bar{X}(r),\bar{X}(T),\bar{Y}(r))\negthinspace+\negthinspace\negthinspace\int_{r}^{T}\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace g^{0}\big(r,s,\bar{X}(r),\bar{X}(s),\bar{\Psi}(s,\bar{X}(s)),\bar{Y}(s),\bar{Z}(s),\bar{Y}^{0}(s),\bar{Z}^{0}(r,s)\big)ds

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{r}^{T}\bar{Z}^{0}(r,s)dW(s),\quad r\in[0,T].

Then by
2.2
, we have the following representation formula:

\displaystyle\bar{Y}(r)=\Theta(r,\bar{X}(r)),\quad\bar{Z}(r)=\Theta_{x}(r,\bar{X}(r))\sigma(r,\bar{X}(r),\bar{\Psi}(r,\bar{X}(r))),

\displaystyle\bar{Y}^{0}(r)=\Theta^{0}\big(r,r,\bar{X}(r),\bar{X}(r),\Theta(r,\bar{X}(r))\big),

\displaystyle\bar{Z}^{0}(r,s)=\Theta^{0}_{x}\big(r,s,\bar{X}(r),\bar{X}(s),\Theta(r,\bar{X}(r))\big)\sigma(s,\bar{X}(s),\bar{\Psi}(s,\bar{X}(s))),

provided the equilibrium HJB equation (
2.18
) admits a classical solution
(\Theta(\cdot),\Theta^{0}(\cdot))
.
Thus, the form of the equilibrium HJB equation (
2.18
) is very natural, though it seems a little bit complicated.
Using the local optimality condition (
2.17
), the equilibrium strategy value
\bar{\Psi}(s,x)
is determined by

\Theta(s,x)
and the diagonal value
\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))
.

2.4
Well-posedness of the Equilibrium HJB Equation

In this subsection, we will present the well-posedness of equation (
2.18
) to some extent.
Note that (
2.18
) is a coupled system of fully nonlinear parabolic PDEs with a non-local feature,
whose well-posedness is a very challenging problem.
Indeed, even for the equilibrium HJB equation associated with the time-inconsistent problems for SDEs (see Yong
[
75
]
),
the well-posedness is still widely open, except in the small time case (see Lei–Pun
[
39
]
).
For the small time case, one can construct a contraction mapping
in a Banach space depending on the terminal conditions and does not need to establish a prior estimate,
which is exactly the main difficulty in establishing the well-posedness.

We now assume that

\sigma(s,x,u)=\sigma(s,x),\quad(s,x,u)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times U.

(2.21)

In this case, we denote

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}(s,x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta})=pb(s,x,u)+g(s,x,u,\theta,p\sigma(s,x)),\qquad(s,x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta})\in{\cal A}[0,T],

\displaystyle{\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0})=p^{0}b(s,x,u)\negthinspace+\negthinspace g^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\theta,p\sigma(s,x),\theta^{0},p^{0}\sigma(s,x)),

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0})={\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0})+q^{0}{\bm{H}}(s,x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta}),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0})\in{\cal A}^{0}[0,T],~q^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times m}.

Then the mapping
\psi(\cdot)
determined by
(H3)
can be determined by the following:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\psi(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0}),\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0})=\inf_{u\in U}\widehat{\bm{H}}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0}),

\displaystyle\forall(t,s,\tilde{x},x,u,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0})\in{\cal A}^{0}[0,T],~q^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times m}.

(2.22)

Namely, in the current case,
\psi(\cdot)
is independent of
P
and
P^{0}
. Then
(
2.18
) is reduced to the following system of semilinear PDEs:

\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta_{s}(s,x)\negthinspace+\negthinspace\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(s,x)a(s,x)]\negthinspace+\negthinspace\Theta_{x}(s,x)b(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad\negthinspace+\negthinspace g(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x),\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x))\negthinspace=\negthinspace 0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta^{0}_{s}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta^{0}_{xx}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)a(s,x)]+\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)b(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad+g^{0}\big(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x),\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\sigma(s,x)\big)=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta(T,x)=h(x),\quad\Theta^{0}(t,T,\tilde{x},x,y)=h^{0}(t,\tilde{x},x,y),\end{array}\right.

(2.23)

with

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}\negthinspace(s,x)\negthinspace=\negthinspace\psi\big(s,s,x,x,\negthinspace\Theta(s,x),\negthinspace\Theta_{x}(s,x),\negthinspace\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\negthinspace\Theta(s,x)),\negthinspace\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\negthinspace\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\negthinspace\Theta(s,x))\big),

\displaystyle(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(2.24)

Now, we denote

\displaystyle\tilde{b}\big(s,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))\big)

\displaystyle\quad:=b\big(s,x,\psi\big(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))\big)\big),

\displaystyle\tilde{g}\big(s,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))\big)

\displaystyle\quad:=g\big(s,x,\psi\big(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))\big),\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x)\big),

\displaystyle\tilde{g}^{0}\big(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),

\displaystyle\qquad\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\big)

\displaystyle\quad:=g^{0}\big(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\psi\big(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))\big),\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x),\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\sigma(s,x)\big).

Because of the above dependence, we may write the above (
2.23
) as follows:

\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\Theta_{s}(s,x)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(s,x)a(s,x)]+\Theta_{x}(s,x)\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad\cdot\tilde{b}\big(s,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))\big)\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad+\tilde{g}\big(s,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))\big)=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta^{0}_{s}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta^{0}_{xx}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)a(s,x)]+\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad\cdot\tilde{b}\big(s,x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))\big)\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad+\tilde{g}^{0}\big(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x),\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\qquad\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x)),\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\big)=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta(T,x)=h(x),\qquad\Theta^{0}(t,T,\tilde{x},x,y)=h^{0}(t,\tilde{x},x,y).\end{array}\right.

(2.25)

Although the above looks complicated, it actually has a usual HJB equation form, which can be abbreviated as follows, if we suppress some lengthy arguments:

\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\Theta_{s}(s,x)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(s,x)a(s,x)]+\Theta_{x}(s,x)\tilde{b}(s,x)+\tilde{g}(s,x)=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta^{0}_{s}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta^{0}_{xx}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)a(s,x)]+\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)\tilde{b}(s,x)+\tilde{g}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta(T,x)=h(x),\qquad\Theta^{0}(t,T,\tilde{x},x,y)=h^{0}(t,\tilde{x},x,y).\end{array}\right.

(2.26)

For the above system, we introduce the following assumption.

(H4)
.

The mappings

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle(s,x)\mapsto a(s,x),\quad x\mapsto h(x),\quad(t,\tilde{x},x,y)\mapsto h^{0}(t,\tilde{x},x,y),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle(s,x,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0})\mapsto\tilde{b}(s,x,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0}),\quad(s,x,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0})\mapsto\tilde{g}(s,x,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0}),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0},\widehat{p}^{0})\mapsto\tilde{g}^{0}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{0},q^{0},\widehat{p}^{0})\end{array}\right.

are bounded, have all required differentiability with bounded derivatives.
Moreover, there exist two constants
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}>0
such that

\lambda_{0}I\leqslant a(t,x)\leqslant\lambda_{1}I,\quad\forall(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
(H4)
hold. Then the equilibrium HJB equation (
2.25
) admits a unique classical solution.

The proof of
Theorem 2.10
is technical and lengthy, which will be given in Section 7. Note that (
2.25
) contains the diagonal term
\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))
.
To our best knowledge, it is the first time that such an equilibrium HJB equation is derived.

3
Comparisons with the Existing Results

3.1
Comparison with Peng
[
52
]

In
[
52
]
, Peng established the dynamic programming principle (DPP, for short) for the optimal control problem with the state equation

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle dX(s)=b(s,X(s),u(s))ds+\sigma(s,X(s),u(s))dW(s),\quad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle dY(s)=-g(s,X(s),u(s),Y(s),Z(s))ds+Z(s)dW(s),\quad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle X(t)=\xi,\quad Y(T)=h(X(T)),\end{array}\right.

(3.1)

and the cost functional

J(t,\xi;u(\cdot))=Y(t),

(3.2)

with the backward process
Y(\cdot)
being one-dimensional. Such a problem is denoted by Problem (R).
It turns out that in this case, the optimal control problem is time-consistent.
The following provides a time-consistency analysis of Problem (R) from the viewpoint of Pontryagin’s maximum principle.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose that
\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(\cdot)
is an optimal control of
Problem (R)
with the initial pair
(t,\xi)\in\mathscr{D}
.
Then
\bar{u}^{t,\xi}(\cdot)
satisfies the necessary condition (
2.9
) for any
\tau\in(t,T]
.

Proof.

By (
2.8
) and the fact that
h^{0}(x,y)=y;(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
and
g^{0}(\cdot)\equiv 0
, we have

\bar{b}_{u}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Y}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{\sigma}_{u}^{t,\xi}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(s)=0,\quad s\in[t,T],

(3.3)

with

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle d\widehat{Y}^{t,\xi}(s)=-\big[\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{x}(s)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{b}^{t,\xi}_{x}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Y}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{\sigma}^{t,\xi}_{x}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)\big]ds+\widehat{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle d\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(s)=\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{y}(s)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(s)ds+\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{z}(s)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(s)dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\widehat{Y}^{t,\xi}(T)=\bar{h}^{t,\xi}_{x}(T)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(T),\quad\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(t)=1.\end{array}\right.

(3.4)

Clearly,
\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(s)
is non-zero for any
s\in[t,T]
. For any
\tau\in(t,T]
, define

\left\{\begin{aligned} \widehat{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)=\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(s)[\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)]^{-1},\quad s\in[\tau,T],\\
\widehat{Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)=\widehat{Y}^{t,\xi}(s)[\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)]^{-1},\quad s\in[\tau,T],\\
\widehat{Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)=\widehat{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)[\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)]^{-1},\quad s\in[\tau,T].\end{aligned}\right.

(3.5)

From (
3.4
), it is easily seen that

(\widehat{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\cdot),\widehat{Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\cdot),\widehat{Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\cdot))

is the unique solution to the following FBSDE on
[\tau,T]
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} d\widehat{Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)&=-\big[\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{x}(s)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)+\bar{b}^{t,\xi}_{x}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)+\bar{\sigma}^{t,\xi}_{x}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)\big]ds\\
&\quad+\widehat{Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)dW(s),\\
d\widehat{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)&=\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{y}(s)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)ds+\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{z}(s)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)dW(s),\\
\widehat{Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(T)&=\bar{h}^{t,\xi}_{x}(T)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(T),\quad\widehat{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(\tau)=1.\end{aligned}\right.

Then by (
3.3
), we get

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)+\bar{b}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Y}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)+\bar{\sigma}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Z}^{\tau,\bar{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)}(s)

\displaystyle\quad=\big[\bar{g}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{b}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Y}^{t,\xi}(s)+\bar{\sigma}^{t,\xi}_{u}(s)^{\top}\widehat{Z}^{t,\xi}(s)\big][\widehat{X}^{t,\xi}(\tau)]^{-1}=0,\quad s\in[\tau,T],~\tau\in[t,T],

which implies the necessary condition (
2.9
) holds.
∎

We remark that Problem (R) is a very special case of Problem (N)
and the relationship (
3.5
) does not hold in general.
When
Y(\cdot)
is multi-dimensional, the problem is time-inconsistent in general,
even if
J(t,\xi;u(\cdot))
is a linear function of
Y(t)
. Here is a simple example.

Example 3.2
.

Consider the (degenerate) FBSDE state equation

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\dot{X}(s)=0,\quad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\dot{Y}_{1}(s)=u(s),\quad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\dot{Y}_{2}(s)=-Y_{1}(s)-u(s)-|u(s)|^{2},\quad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle X(t)=x,\quad Y_{1}(T)=0,\quad Y_{2}(T)=0,\end{array}\right.

with the cost functional

J(t,x;u(\cdot))=Y_{2}(t).

Then

\displaystyle J(t,x;u(\cdot))

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{T}[Y_{1}(s)+u(s)+|u(s)|^{2}]ds=\int_{t}^{T}\Big[u(s)+|u(s)|^{2}-\int_{s}^{T}u(r)dr\Big]ds

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{T}\Big[(1+t-s)u(s)+|u(s)|^{2}\Big]ds.

Thus, the unique optimal control
\bar{u}(\cdot\,;t,x)
for initial pair
(t,x)
is given by

\bar{u}(s)\equiv\bar{u}(s;t,x)={s-t-1\over 2},\qquad s\in[t,T].

And for any
0\leqslant t<\tau\leqslant T
, the optimal control at
(\tau,\bar{X}(\tau))\equiv(\tau,x)
is given by

\tilde{u}(s)\equiv\tilde{u}(s;\tau,\bar{X}(\tau))={s-\tau-1\over 2},\qquad s\in[\tau,T].

Clearly,

\bar{u}(s)\neq\tilde{u}(s),\quad s\in[\tau,T],

which implies that the problem is time-inconsistent.

We now show that in the case of (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) (with
m=1
),
the equilibrium HJB equation (
2.18
) is reduced to the classical HJB equation
associated with recursive stochastic optimal control problems.
In fact, the associated equilibrium HJB equation is given as follows

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\Theta_{s}(s,x)+\Theta_{x}(s,x)b(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(s,x)a(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))]\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad+g(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x),\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x)))=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta^{0}_{s}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)+\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)b(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta^{0}_{xx}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)a(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))]=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta(T,x)=h(x),\qquad\Theta^{0}(t,y,z,T,x)=y,\end{array}\right.

(3.6)

where
\bar{\Psi}(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfies the local optimality condition (
2.22
).
Clearly,
\Theta^{0}(\cdot)\equiv y
is a classical solution to the second PDE in (
3.6
).
Thus, the local optimality condition (
2.22
) can be rewritten as follows:
For any
(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\Theta_{x}(s,x)b(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))\negthinspace+\negthinspace\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(s,x)a(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))]\negthinspace+\negthinspace g(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x),\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x)))

\displaystyle\quad=\inf_{u\in U}\Big\{\Theta_{x}(s,x)b(s,x,u)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(s,x)a(s,x,u)]+g(s,x,u,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x,u))\Big\}.

Then the equilibrium value function can be given by

\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))=\Theta(s,x),\qquad(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

with
\Theta(\cdot\,,\cdot)
being uniquely determined by

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\Theta_{s}(s,x)+\inf_{u\in U}\Big\{\Theta_{x}(s,x)b(s,x,u)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(s,x)a(s,x,u)],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+g(s,x,u,\Theta(s,x),\Theta_{x}(s,x)\sigma(s,x,u))\Big\}=0,\quad(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta(T,x)=h(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\end{array}\right.

which is exactly the classical HJB equation derived by Peng
[
52
]
.

3.2
Comparison with Yong
[
75
,
77
]
, Wei–Yong–Yu
[
70
]
, and Wang–Yong
[
66
]

As an equilibrium recursive version of
[
75
,
77
,
70
]
,
Wang–Yong
[
66
]
considered the optimal control problems with the state equation

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle dX(s)=b(s,X(s),u(s))ds+\sigma(s,X(s),u(s))dW(s),\quad s\in[t,T],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle X(t)=\xi,\end{array}\right.

(3.7)

and the cost functional

J(t,\xi;u(\cdot))=Y^{0}(t),

(3.8)

where
Y^{0}(\cdot)
is uniquely determined by the following BSVIE:

Y^{0}(r)=h^{0}(r,X(T))+\int_{r}^{T}g^{0}(r,s,X(s),u(s),Y^{0}(s),Z^{0}(r,s))ds-\int_{r}^{T}Z^{0}(r,s)dW(s),\quad r\in[t,T].

(3.9)

Then by comparing the above with (
1.2
) and (
1.3
),
we see that in our problem, the cost functional can additionally depend on the initial state
X(r)

and the backward process
(Y(\cdot),Z(\cdot))
.
If the diffusion term of (
3.7
) does not depend on the control
u(\cdot)
,
the associated equilibrium HJB equation admits the followng form:

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\Theta^{0}_{s}(t,s,x)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta^{0}_{xx}(t,s,x)a(s,x)]+\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,x)\tilde{b}\big(s,x,\Theta^{0}(s,s,x),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x)\big)\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\quad+\tilde{g}^{0}\big(t,s,x,\Theta^{0}(s,s,x),\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x),\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,s,x)\big)=0,\quad(t,s)\in\Delta^{*}[0,T],~x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta^{0}(t,T,x)=h^{0}(t,x),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{array}\right.

(3.10)

Compared with the equilibrium HJB equation (
3.10
) derived in
[
75
,
70
,
66
]
,
(
2.25
) has the following new features:

\bullet

Equilibrium HJB equation (
2.25
) is a coupled system of parabolic PDEs.
It is interesting that the last PDE in (
2.25
) is coupled with the first
m
equations
not only through the appearance of
\Theta(s,x)
and
\Theta_{x}(s,x)
in the function
g^{0}(\cdot)
,
but also through the non-local form
\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))
,

\Theta_{x}^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))
,
\Theta_{y}^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))
of the unknown function
\Theta^{0}(\cdot)
.

\bullet

Equilibrium HJB equation (
2.25
) depends on the partial derivative
\Theta^{0}_{y}(t,s,\tilde{x},x,y)

along the “diagonal” points
(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))
,
by which we see that the backward controlled equation has a significant influence on deducing the equilibrium HJB equation.
To be more clear, we take a look at this from a probabilistic viewpoint. By the Itô’s formula, the stochastic system associated with (
2.25
) reads

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle X(t)=x\negthinspace+\negthinspace\negthinspace\int_{0}^{t}\negthinspace\tilde{b}(s,X(s),Y(s),Z(s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1},Y^{0}(s),Z^{0}(s,s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1},\widehat{Y}^{0}(s))ds\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\negthinspace\negthinspace\int_{0}^{t}\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\sigma(s,X(s))dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle Y(t)\negthinspace=\negthinspace h(X(T))\negthinspace+\negthinspace\negthinspace\int_{t}^{T}\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace\tilde{g}(s,X(s),Y(s),Z(s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1},Y^{0}(s),Z^{0}(s,s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1}\negthinspace\negthinspace,\widehat{Y}^{0}(s))ds\\
\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\negthinspace\negthinspace\int_{t}^{T}\negthinspace\negthinspace\negthinspace Z(s)dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle Y^{0}(t)=h^{0}(t,X(t),X(T),Y(t))+\int_{t}^{T}\widetilde{g}^{0}\big(t,s,X(t),X(s),Y(s),Z(s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1},Y^{0}(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\qquad\qquad Z^{0}(s,s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1},\widehat{Y}^{0}(s),Z^{0}(t,s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1}\big)ds-\int_{t}^{T}Z^{0}(t,s)dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\widehat{Y}^{0}(t)=h^{0}_{y}(t,X(t),X(T),Y(t))-\int_{t}^{T}\widehat{Z}^{0}(t,s)dW(s)+\int_{t}^{T}\widetilde{g}^{0}_{p^{0}}\big(t,s,X(t),X(s),Y(s),Z(s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad Y^{0}(s),Z^{0}(s,s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1},\widehat{Y}^{0}(s),Z^{0}(t,s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1}\big)Z^{0}(t,s)\sigma(s,X(s))^{-1}ds.\end{array}\right.

Compared with
[
66
, Theorem 5.1]
, the first backward equation and the third backward equation are new.
The appearance of the first backward equation is natural, because the state system (
1.1
) is a controlled FBSDE.
However, the appearance of the third backward equation is surprising.
Indeed, the process
\widehat{Y}^{0}(\cdot)
is introduced for providing a probabilistic representation for the term
\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,\Theta(s,x),x,s,x)
,
which comes from the local optimality condition of the Hamiltonian (
2.22
).

\bullet

In (
2.25
), there are three diagonal/non-local terms
\Theta^{0}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))
,
\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))
and
\Theta^{0}_{y}(s,s,x,x,\Theta(s,x))
. Equilibrium HJB equation (
2.25
) is non-local not only in the time variables
(t,s)
(as in
[
75
,
77
,
70
,
66
]
) but also in the space variables
(\tilde{x},x,y)
.
More interestingly, at the variable
y
, the diagonal/non-local terms are obtained by setting
y=\Theta(s,x)
,
instead of by letting
y=x
. Thus, the non-locality of (
2.25
) is much more complicated than the ones derived in
[
75
,
77
,
70
,
66
]
.
We need to make some very careful analysis in establishing the well-posedness.

3.3
Comparison with Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]

In
[
7
]
, Björk, Khapko, and Murgoci
considered the optimal control problems with the state equation

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle dX(s)=b(s,X(s),u(s))ds+\sigma(s,X(s),u(s))dW(s),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle X(t)=\xi,\end{array}\right.

and the cost functional

J(t,x;u(\cdot))=\mathbb{E}_{t}[\widehat{F}(X(t),X(T))]+\widehat{G}(X(t),\mathbb{E}_{t}[X(T)]),

where
\widehat{F}(\cdot)
and
\widehat{G}(\cdot)
are given deterministic functions.
The so-called
extended HJB equation
derived by Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
reads

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\inf_{u\in U}\Big(({\bf A}^{u}\widehat{V})(t,x)-({\bf A}^{u}\widehat{f})(t,x,x)+({\bf A}^{u}\widehat{f}^{x})(t,x)-{\bf A}^{u}(\widehat{G}\diamond\widehat{g})(t,x)+({\bf H}^{u}\widehat{g})(t,x)\Big)=0,\\
&{\bf A}^{\widehat{u}}\widehat{f}^{\tilde{x}}(t,x)=0,\quad{\bf A}^{\widehat{u}}\widehat{g}(t,x)=0,\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
&\widehat{V}(T,x)=\widehat{F}(x,x)+\widehat{G}(x,x),\quad\widehat{f}^{\tilde{x}}(T,x)=\widehat{F}(\tilde{x},x),\quad\widehat{g}(T,x)=x,\quad x,\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\end{aligned}\right.

(3.11)

where
\widehat{u}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the strategy which realizes the infimum in the first equation; that is

\displaystyle({\bf A}^{\widehat{u}}\widehat{V})(t,x)-({\bf A}^{\widehat{u}}\widehat{f})(t,x,x)+({\bf A}^{\widehat{u}}\widehat{f}^{x})(t,x)-{\bf A}^{\widehat{u}}(\widehat{G}\diamond\widehat{g})(t,x)+({\bf H}^{\widehat{u}}\widehat{g})(t,x)

\displaystyle\quad=\inf_{u\in U}\Big(({\bf A}^{u}\widehat{V})(t,x)-({\bf A}^{u}\widehat{f})(t,x,x)+({\bf A}^{u}\widehat{f}^{x})(t,x)-{\bf A}^{u}(\widehat{G}\diamond\widehat{g})(t,x)+({\bf H}^{u}\widehat{g})(t,x)\Big).

(3.12)

In the above, the following notations are used

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(t,x,\tilde{x})=\widehat{f}^{\tilde{x}}(t,x),\quad(\widehat{G}\diamond\widehat{g})(t,x)=\widehat{G}(x,\widehat{g}(t,x)),\quad{\bf H}^{u}\widehat{g}(t,x)=\widehat{G}_{y}(s,\widehat{g}(t,x)){\bf A}^{u}\widehat{g}(t,x),

and the operator
{\bf A}^{u}
is determined by

\mathbb{E}_{t}[k(t+h,X(t+h;t,x,u))]=k(t,x)+h{\bf A}^{u}k(t,x)+o(h),\quad\forall k(\cdot\,,\cdot)\in C^{1,2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}),

(3.13)

where
X(\cdot\,;t,x,u)
is the unique solution to the forward equation in (
1.1
).

The associated equilibrium HJB equation (
2.18
) reads

\left\{\negthinspace\negthinspace\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\Theta_{s}(s,x)+\Theta_{x}(s,x)b(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(s,x)a(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))]=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta^{0}_{s}(s,\tilde{x},x,y)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta^{0}_{xx}(s,\tilde{x},x,y)a(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))]+\Theta^{0}_{x}(s,\tilde{x},x,y)b(s,x,\bar{\Psi}(s,x))=0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\displaystyle\Theta(T,x)=x,\quad\Theta^{0}(T,\tilde{x},x,y)=\widehat{F}(\tilde{x},x)+\widehat{G}(\tilde{x},y),\end{array}\right.

(3.14)

where
\bar{\Psi}(\cdot\,,\cdot)
satisfies the
local optimality condition
:

\displaystyle\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,x,x,\Theta(t,x))b(t,x,\bar{\Psi}(t,x))+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta^{0}_{xx}(t,x,x,\Theta(t,x))a(t,x,\bar{\Psi}(t,x))]

\displaystyle\qquad+\Theta^{0}_{y}(t,x,x,\Theta(t,x))\big\{\Theta_{x}(t,x)b(t,x,\bar{\Psi}(t,x))+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(t,x)a(t,x,\bar{\Psi}(t,x))]\big\}

\displaystyle\quad=\inf_{u\in U}\Big\{\Theta^{0}_{x}(t,x,x,\Theta(t,x))b(t,x,u)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta^{0}_{xx}(t,x,x,\Theta(t,x))a(t,x,u)]

\displaystyle\qquad+\Theta^{0}_{y}(t,x,x,\Theta(t,x))\big\{\Theta_{x}(t,x)b(t,x,u)+\hbox{tr$\,$}[\Theta_{xx}(t,x)a(t,x,u)]\big\}\Big\},\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(3.15)

Now we compare the equilibrium HJB equation (
3.14
) derived in the paper
with the extended HJB equation (
3.11
) obtained by
Björk–Khapko–Murgoci
[
7
]
carefully.

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose that the equilibrium HJB equation (
3.14
) admits a classical solution
